Godzilla Minus Zero Ending Explained: Where The Franchise Goes From Here

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Full spoilers for "Godzilla Minus Zero" follow.

For the longest time in the character's 72-year history, the "Godzilla" films were surprisingly light on continuity. During the Showa era films produced by Toho Studios during the 1950s through the 1970s, the most common recurring characters in the movies were the kaiju themselves. When the Heisei era occurred between 1984 and 1996, they established a continuity that was a bit more contiguous, but not much. The Millennium era became even more piecemeal continuity-wise, with most entries using the original 1954 "Godzilla" as a starting point but erasing everything else until the latest film (similar to how the "Halloween" franchise continuity has operated before). Even the MonsterVerse films produced by Legendary Pictures function most similarly to the Heisei era in terms of continuity, while Toho's 2016 "Shin Godzilla" is basically a one-shot.

All of this is to say that the "Godzilla" franchise is wildly unpredictable where canon is concerned, with each film or groups of films essentially treating it piecemeal. It's exceedingly rare that a true sequel has been made to a "Godzilla" film in the past, where a prior movie's cast and filmmakers have returned for a second round. That milestone has finally been achieved with "Godzilla Minus Zero."

The movie is an unabashed sequel to 2023's "Godzilla Minus One," as it's once again written and directed by Takashi Yamazaki. As such, it's a beautifully rich experience previously unseen in most "Godzilla" movies, one which builds from its predecessor in a big way. "Minus Zero" isn't just another "Godzilla" adventure, but a movie which continues the "Minus" series' character arcs and themes, and its emotional, somewhat cliffhanger-style conclusion indicates that the story may not be over yet.

"Godzilla Minus One" takes place between 1945 and 1947, during the immediate aftermath of World War II. Japan's loss of the war has left it hurting in more ways than one, with the country having to muddle through dire straits both physical and emotional. This is represented by the ensemble of characters we meet, chief among them Kōichi Shikishima (Ryunosuke Kamiki), a kamikaze pilot who deserts his suicidal duty, Noriko Ōishi (Minami Hamabe), a woman who takes it upon herself to rescue and raise an orphan girl, Akiko (Sae Nagatani), and Kenji Noda (Hidetaka Yoshioka), a Naval weapons engineer.

All three adults come to view the superpowered creature Godzilla as emblematic of their personal struggles. For Noda, Godzilla must be stopped for Japan to have a future. For Noriko, Godzilla is something to be survived. For Shikishima, Godzilla is the personification of his guilt, as well as a force of death and destruction who must be vanquished.

After Noriko's apparent demise during a Godzilla attack on Ginza, Noda spearheads a civilian-run mission in which a couple of Navy destroyers will alternately sink and raise Godzilla at dangerous speeds. As a failsafe, Shikishima has volunteered to fly one of the last available Shinden fighters to lure Godzilla out to sea. However, his real plan is to fly the plane, loaded with explosives, directly into Godzilla's mouth. He does so, but uses an ejection seat to escape at the last minute, proving he's overcome his guilt and has decided to live. Following the Godzilla victory, Shikishima learns that Noriko has in fact survived, and he and Akiko go to visit her in the hospital. As Noriko tearfully reunites with her new family, a mysterious black mark on her neck shimmers.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" picks up in 1949, when Noda is informed of another giant monster attacking Japan: Ghidorah. As Noda and his new advisor, meterologist Makoto Aoyama (Masami Nagasawa), plan to kill Ghidorah with lightning, Noda asks Shikishima for help. Shikishima tries to stay out of the battle for the sake of his now-wife Noriko and adopted daughter Akiko, especially as he and Noriko are the only ones who know that she survived Ginza due to being infected with Godzilla's cells, which might lead to her death soon.

Yet when Ghidorah survives the initial attack and a now-regenerated Godzilla reappears, Noda and biologist Kanji Murakami (Min Tanaka), a survivor of the Hiroshima bombing, decide the best plan is to lure Godzilla and Ghidorah into a deathmatch fight, with the humans killing the victor. To do this, Murakami has a device which means only Noriko and her G cells can "call" Godzilla.

Shikishima and Noriko agree to the plan, unaware that Murakami intends to later use Noriko as a way to get revenge on the USA. Godzilla and Ghidorah fight in Ginza, and the former eventually triumphs, though at the expense of Noda's life. When the Japanese army gives orders that Godzilla is not to be killed, Shikishima and Noriko mutiny against Murakami. However, Murakami reveals that Noda recently convinced him of the error of his ways, and the couple are allowed to escape while the scientist destroys his machine and his work.

Unfortunately, Murakami's good deed is punished when the US army arrests him. Meanwhile, Shikishima and Noriko try to abandon Akiko, knowing they're doomed to a life on the run from the authorities. Yet they ultimately can't leave her behind, and ride off as a family to an uncertain future.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is a true second chapter in a continuing story. This is a fairly unprecedented occurrence in the Godzilla franchise, not just in the way the returning characters are built upon and expanded, but also in the way Takashi Yamazaki continues the themes from "Minus One." Rather than jumping ahead in time too far, "Minus Zero" is still a story of post-war Japan, and nowhere is this more evident than in Kanji Murakami's character.

Although Godzilla is shifted toward a more heroic light, as has happened many times over the franchise, he's still presented as morally dubious. If, in "Minus One," Godzilla stood for the sins of the Japanese (if not all humanity) come to punish them, "Minus Zero" looks at Godzilla as the country's answer to the atomic bomb: a destructive and ultimately uncontrollable weapon which should only be used as a last resort, if ever.

In addition to the war allegory, "Godzilla Minus Zero" takes the characters of "Minus One" beyond their absolution in that film. Much of "Minus Zero" concerns whether the happy (or relatively happy) future the survivors of that movie fought so hard for can even happen, as everything from the Ghidorah attacks to the potentially deadly G Cells in Noriko's body appears like an insurmountable threat. Yet while unfortunate consequences are unavoidable, Yamazaki's optimism ultimately shines through, especially in the way the bonds between the characters mean that all hope is never lost. Even the supporting cast from "Minus One," though given smaller roles here, contribute to this theme. The implication is that Japan, if not humanity, can have a bright future if everyone supports each other and serves their better natures rather than being selfish or hateful.

If "Godzilla Minus Zero" were to conclude the "Minus" continuity, then both "Godzilla Minus One" and "Minus Zero" would serve as a fully satisfying tale. Still, Takashi Yamazaki does leave "Minus Zero" on several cliffhangers, or at least unresolved questions. The ending leaves the ultimate fate of many characters uncertain, chief among them Murakami and the Shikishima family. In the case of the former, it seems likely that the US army intend to make Murakami work for them to get Godzilla under American control.

In the case of the latter, the Shikishimas are now going to be on the run, and it's unclear what their plan is to evade the government(s). It's neat how these films have set up so much good material with the human characters that there's enough interest in what happens to them before one even considers the kaiju.

But oh, the giant monsters! We cannot forget about them. Now that Yamazaki has introduced Ghidorah, the door is open for any other Toho-verse kaiju to turn up. In terms of the themes that the "Minus" films are dealing with, it would feel appropriate for other heavily allegorical beasts like Rodan or Hedorah to make their appearance. Although the MonsterVerse's "Godzilla vs. Kong" recently used Mechagodzilla as humanity's warmongering response to Godzilla, such a characterization would fit snugly into the "Minus" films, too. And, while "Minus Zero" only contains a fantasy sequence of Godzilla attacking NYC, there's the chance that the action could expand beyond Japan for real. While it's not clear whether there will be another "Minus" film at all yet, there's certainly no dearth of possibilities for one to explore.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is in theaters now.

On Oct. 14, Mexico amended a 2020 gillnet fishing regulation, reducing the prohibited gillnet fishing area and protected vaquita habitat by thousands of square miles, or by 86% of its previous area.

“The government made a decision with potentially catastrophic consequences for the vaquita without giving the public a meaningful chance to examine the proposed boundaries and respond,â said Alex Olivera, Mexico representative at the Center for Biological Diversity in a Center for Biological Diversity press release Friday.

âWith only about 10 of these little porpoises left, Mexico should be enforcing rules in the vaquitaâs entire habitat, not legalizing gillnets in waters that should be protected. Vaquitas donât follow maps, and scientists have repeatedly seen them outside the zero tolerance area.â

Also on Friday, in an apparent response to the lawsuit, the Mexican government issued a vow to protect the vaquita with the help of surveillance, fisheries management and combatting illegal trafficking of species with economic alternatives for coastal communities.

“The strategy links environmental conservation to social justice: protecting marine ecosystems and generating opportunities for populations that have historically depended on their natural resources. To this end, it promotes sustainable fishing and community participation in decisions on the development of the region,” the Mexican government said.

Though fishermen do not fish for vaquita, the small mammals are vulnerable to being caught in gillnets used by fishermen in Baja used to catch shrimp and illegally fish for wild totoaba.

Totoaba are an endangered fish also endemic to Baja California and are prized for their bladders known as “buche” in the Chinese black market, where they are worth hundreds of thousands of dollars.

The environmental groups claim the Mexican government failed to commit to their environmental commitments, specifically citing the the United Nations EscazÃº Agreement, which Mexico signed in September 2018.

The agreement “aims to guarantee the full and effective implementation in Latin America and the Caribbean of the rights of access to environmental information, public participation in the environmental decision-making process and access to justice in environmental matters.”

Gillnet fishing regulations have been a point of contention for environmental groups and not just recently.

In their 2017 report â one of nine lengthy reports â the International Committee for the Recovery of the Vaquita welcomed the Mexican government’s 2017 agreement that banned commercial gillnet fishing in the Upper Gulf of Baja California but deemed it insufficient, urging a permanent and effective gillnet ban.

They argued the ban did not prohibit the sale or possession of gillnets on land and at sea in the area of the gillnet ban, leading to illegal gillnet fishing.

In response to the committee’s findings and because the vaquita are an endangered species under the U.S. Endangered Species Act, the U.S. banned the “importation of commercial fish or products from fish which have been caught with commercial fishing technology which results in the incidental kill or incidental serious injury of ocean mammals in excess of United States standards.”

âNever before in American history has the federal government, on the eve of an election, sought to coerce a state and local government to abandon their own Constitutional elections procedures and bend to the will of the executive branch,âÂ Carter said. âIn bringing this lawsuit, the executive would have this court believe that it reigns as a sole sovereign. The governmentâs request, the judge added,Â violates longstanding constitutional principles mandating states have control over their elections. IfÂ thisÂ last-minute request were granted, either party could create chaos for their own political purposes to disrupt the election process, he said.

In addition, CarterÂ questioned the credibility of the list of purported noncitizens on Californiaâs voter rolls that Homeland Security compiled, noting a similar list of ineligible voters in Nevada that started with about 16,000 names whittled down to just 185 registered voters who all turned out to be citizens.

Days after Carter’s ruling, the Ninth Circuit in a split decision siding with him.

âWe decline the United Statesâ invitation to contravene the elections clause by undermining the Constitutionâs plain text,â the panel wrote then in a per curiam opinion. âIf the United States believes different procedures are necessary for vote-by-mail ballots, we believe it should take that suggestion to Congress, not the courts.â

While U.S. Circuit Judge Mark Bennett, a Donald Trump appointee, and U.S. Circuit Judge Gabriel Sanchez, a Joe Biden appointee, denied the governmentâs appeal, U.S. Circuit Judge Bumatay, also a Trump appointee, dissented, as he had done in the previous ruling.

âThroughout history, federal courts have acted to protect the voting and civil rights of our citizens by enjoining state action that violates the law,â Bumatay wrote. âAnd common sense also tells us that the federal government shouldnât have to sit on its hands while a state allows the violation of federal law, especially when the integrity of federal elections is at stake.â

The U.S. Supreme Court summarily rejected the Trumpâs administration emergency request to interfere in California’s election without even requiring a response from the state. The justices did not explain their decision and there were no noted dissents.

In a statement, Jill Habig, founder and CEO of the Public Rights Project, called the dismissal a victory for all local elections officials.

“The federal government never should have brought this lawsuit. Now, having lost at every level, they finally recognized there was no more case left to pursue,” she said.

U.S. District Judge Trina Thompson had blocked the department last week from threatening, intimidating or coercing local election officials nationwide from processing ballots of registered voters the government suspects belong to noncitizens. 📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

(CN) âÂ A federal judge on Friday dismissed class action claims by two Utah men who say their mental and sexual health was damaged after they were exposed to pornography at an early age on Pornhub.

The plaintiffs, both men in their early 20s, accused Aylo Global Entertainment â the Montreal-based company behind Pornhub â and its subsidiaries of criminal child abuse by exposing a generation of American children to hardcore pornography on its websites for profit.

But U.S. District Judge David Barlow found that the two men failed to establish the company could be sued in Utah.

âMerely listing three Utah laws without analyzing how specific factual allegations in the amended complaint plausibly demonstrate each was violated is insufficient,â the Donald Trump appointee wrote. âThus, plaintiffs have not met their burden to plead jurisdiction under Mashaâs Law.â

Mashaâs Law is a federal statute that allows victims of child sexual abuse material to sue for damages.

Barlow, a Donald Trump appointee, added that just because the defendants operated a business and website that reached Utah residents, the court did not necessarily have personal jurisdiction over a defendant that was not based in the forum state.

The plaintiffs, identified only by their initials, said the defendants are liable for product liability, negligence and knowingly engaging in a sexual act with minors, among many other claims.

âDefendantsâ products stole their childhoods, leaving them addicted to material that warped their understanding of romantic love and isolating them in a world of shame and secrets for years,â the plaintiffs wrote in their March complaint.

The plaintiffs had argued that the court had jurisdiction over the non-Utah defendants because of their connection to the plaintiffs in Utah. The plaintiffs say that Aylo substantially engaged with Utah residents through marketing, advertising, promoting subscription services, maintaining web traffic and collecting user data.

âA website operator must do more than passively maintain a website that can be accessed in the forum state to subject itself to jurisdiction,â he wrote.

An action aimed at a forum state must also do more than just involve residents, he ruled. Otherwise, website operators could be brought into any court, rendering territorial limits of personal jurisdiction meaningless, he said.

In light of this, he ruled, the plaintiffs failed to meet the âminimum contactsâ test.

âThe court cannot accept conclusory statements that the test is met based on cursory cites,â he wrote. âMore is required from the party that bears the burden.â

Barlow dismissed the complaint without prejudice. He did not decide whether Aylo was liable for those injuries.

In addition to Pornhub, the defendants also operate other major adult websites, including YouPorn, RedTube and Tube8.

âThese platforms are part of defendantsâ extensive network of free platforms, which collectively dominate a significant portion of the American and global online pornography market,â the plaintiffs said.

These websites generate about half a billion dollars in annual revenue, about 50% of which is based in the United States, they claimed.

Aylo Freesites Ltd., one of the defendants, is also suing the Utah state government over its age verification law for adult websites, which passed earlier this year. Barlow is also presiding over that case.

It’s not the first time that Aylo has been challenged by the Utah government.

An attorney from Venable LLP, the law firm representing Aylo Global Holdings, declined to comment.

Attorneys for the plaintiffs did not respond to requests for comment.

The judge, a Joe Biden appointee, last year rejected MindGeekâs argument that it was immune from the womenâs claims under Section 230 of Communication Decency Act, which shields websites from liability over the content its users post.

Although MindGeekâs attorney argued at the time that Pornhub is no different than other porn sites that have had similar claims dismissed, the judge observed that the women in the lawsuits specifically accuse the siteâs moderators of helping users post child pornography and making it easy for other users to find.

Section 230, the judge observed, is a robust protection for online platforms against third-party malfeasance.

âIt is not intended to protect MindGeek from its own malfeasance,â Hsu said. âThat is what is alleged here.â

The Port That Does Not Exist

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Port 0 is the port you are not supposed to use, so nobody looks there. So we put an IRC network on it. Then I got curious whether anyone else had, and scanned all of IPv4 for port 0. Turns out we weren't alone, and some of our neighbours really shouldn't be there.

Ask any program to listen on port 0 and it will hand you a socket on port 47193...or 58037...or whatever the kernel felt like that day. Port 0 is not a port you can have. It is the number you pass when you want the operating system to pick one for you.

But a TCP header is just bytes. The destination port field is 16 bits wide, and 0x0000 fits in 16 bits as well as anything else does. Nothing on the wire stops a packet from being addressed to port 0. Your tools will refuse it, your browser will block it, and your scanner's default port list skips it, because everyone agrees it isn't a real port.

Which makes it a pretty good place to hide things, whether anyone meant to or not. So we did some TCP cowboy shit & port scanned the entire fucking internet for open ports on port 0, which uncovered some interesting unexpected findings.

The standards are thin on this. RFC 9293, the 2022 consolidation of the TCP spec, defines the source and destination port as "16 bits" and leaves it there. It has no rule against zero.

IANA lists 0/tcp and 0/udp as Reserved, pointing at RFC 6335. That RFC splits the port space into System (0–1023), User (1024–49151) and Dynamic (49152–65535) ranges, and explains what "Reserved" means:

So port 0 is not forbidden. It is set aside. The only place zero has a defined protocol meaning is UDP: RFC 768 makes the source port optional, and "if not used, a value of zero is inserted." TCP has nothing like that.

Port 0's reputation comes from the sockets API, not the wire. When you call bind() with sin_port = 0, you are not asking for port 0. You are asking for any port. The ip(7) man page lists every case where Linux hands out an ephemeral port: bind() with port 0, listen() on an unbound socket, connect() on an unbound socket, and sendto() on an unbound datagram socket.

The range comes from net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range, which defaults to 32768 60999 on Linux, not the 49152–65535 that IANA calls Dynamic. And asking for port 0 needs no privileges. __inet_bind() only checks CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE when the requested port is non-zero.

How the kernel picks is more interesting than I expected. In net/ipv4/inet_connection_sock.c (v6.8), inet_csk_find_open_port() starts at a random offset and then does this:

It walks the range in steps of two. The function above it says it outright: "We try to allocate an odd port (and leave even ports for connect())." Outgoing connections go through __inet_hash_connect() in inet_hashtables.c, which implements RFC 6056 Algorithm 4 (double-hash port selection) and takes the even ports first. The kernel splits the range by parity so servers asking for "any port" and clients making outbound connections don't fight over the same numbers. If SO_REUSEADDR is set, bind() also tries the lower half of the range before the upper half.

That is easy to test. On a stock Ubuntu box running Linux 6.8:

So when your program says "listen on port 0", the kernel hears "give me an odd number between 32768 and 61000." No normal program can listen on port 0.

You can bypass all of this with raw sockets, but if you just want to cheat, you can simply forward the port to another with iptables/nftables. As a fun proof of concept, I set up my IRC network at irc.supernets.org to accept plaintext connections on port 0 and SSL connections on port 1. JUST TO FLEX. ð

A SYN arrives for port 0, netfilter rewrites the destination to 6667 where the ircd is actually listening, and the reply is rewritten back on the way out. The ircd never knows. Port 1 does the same for TLS on 6697.

Getting a server onto port 0 was the easy part. Getting a client there is where tools give up. I pointed everything I had at our own server on port 0:

That last one is strange, because the TCP connection underneath it is fine. The problem is that the kernel says it isn't, and it does that on purpose. Here is inet_getname() in net/ipv4/af_inet.c, which backs getpeername():

Linux uses "destination port is 0" as its internal marker for "this socket isn't connected to anything." A socket that really is connected to port 0 trips that check, so getpeername() returns ENOTCONN on a working connection.

Python's ssl.wrap_socket() calls getpeername() to decide whether the socket is connected, gets ENOTCONN, decides it isn't, switches to non-blocking and tries a recv(1), gets EAGAIN, and raises BlockingIOError. The TCP connection is fine. It just reports that it doesn't exist. Python's asyncio does TLS through memory buffers instead of wrap_socket(), never asks, and works.

You can't scan port 0 with connect()-based tools for all the reasons above, but masscan doesn't use the kernel's TCP stack. It builds its own SYN packets, so port 0 is just another number.

1,173,626 addresses answered with a SYN-ACK.

That sounds like a lot of people using port 0. It isn't. Grouped by /24, the responses are packed into a few thousand dense blocks:

8% of those /24s hold 78% of the responding IPs. Whole blocks answer on every address: 172.65.0.0/16 returned 65,534 of 65,536. That isn't 65,534 servers. It's a firewall or router at the edge answering the SYN for everything behind it. 1,116,675 addresses completed the handshake and never sent a single byte.

56,951 addresses did send something. Almost all of it looked like this:

F5 BIG-IP load balancers write the reason for a reset into the RST packet, and masscan saved it as a banner. That's 53,741 of the 56,951 (94%), and 46,583 of them are in 132.246.0.0/16 alone. Drop those, plus 305 hosts returning the same fixed (Ref.Id: ?...?) token, and 2,905 addresses are left. That's about 0.25% of everything that answered.

Nobody is running anything exotic on port 0. It's the same stuff that's on every other port: SSH, databases, telnet, FTP. What stands out is how much of it is embedded gear. dropbear, micro_httpd, NAS certificates from QNAP, Synology and Buffalo, DVRs, power switches. Some of it clearly comes from one device model rolled out across an ISP:

My guess is that nobody at those ISPs picked port 0 on purpose: a port-forward or NAT rule shipped in a CPE config with the external port left at zero, and the device honored it. Then there are the hosting providers. GigaCloud (Ukraine), VDCBY (Belarus) and SiS Distribution (Thailand) have SSH on port 0 with 18, 13 and 10 different SSH version strings. Different builds means different machines, so these are individual customers, not one firmware image.

GigaCloud also accounts for 16 of the 80 mail servers on port 0, mostly Ukrainian organisations: mail.dtek-kem.com.ua (DTEK, the Ukrainian energy company), smtp01.health24.ua, mx.eastenergy.com.ua, mail.citrus.com.ua, and two government servers, mail.depfin.dn.gov.ua and mail.sed-rada.gov.ua. Why they all share this setup, I don't know. Elsewhere in mail: a Uruguayan government host, prod-carbonium01.rionegro.gub.uy, and mssante.elsan.care (62.106.147.123), a submission server for MSSantÃ©, the French national secure health messaging system, run by the private hospital group Elsan. When masscan poked it, it replied:

The rest of the router logins look the same as they would on port 80. A D-Link DSL-2740B (37.103.36.12 and two others), with "admin" already filled in as the username: two in French at Orange and Nerim in France, one in English at Wind Tre in Italy. A Linksys "Router Access" page in El Salvador (200.31.162.80). A plain "Sign in" form titled "Router Admin" on seven cloud servers: four at Hetzner, two at Online SAS, one at DigitalOcean.

One didn't bother with a login at all. A 3G/4G "Wireless Mobile Router" on China Unicom (183.93.33.155) serves its full status page on port 0 to anyone who asks: its WAN IP, LAN, WAN and wireless MAC addresses, SSID, firmware build, uptime, memory usage and whether DHCP is on.

Browsers won't open a URL with :0 in it, so to see the web interfaces I ran a small relay: listen on a local port, connect out to the target on port 0, pipe the bytes through, and point headless Chromium at the local port. 160 addresses speak HTTP on port 0, and 85 rendered a page.

A Gira HomeServer 4 (46.186.48.86), a KNX home and building automation controller, with its start page, debug page and monitor links sitting on port 0.

A Trane Tracer Synchrony login (201.171.202.25), which is HVAC and building control.

The web pages were the easy part to read. The more interesting devices don't speak HTTP. They send binary the moment you connect, and figuring out what they are meant matching bytes against nmap's probe database, Wireshark dissectors and, in one case, a disassembled vendor client.

A Beckhoff PLC with its screen open to anyone. 46.178.29.102, on Proximus in Belgium, sends this on connect:

The CX5120 is a Beckhoff embedded PC running Windows CE, the kind that runs TwinCAT and drives a machine. The protocol is CERDISP, Windows CE Remote Display, which Beckhoff's own security guideline lists as open on TCP 987 by default. I disassembled Beckhoff's client, CERHOST.exe 2.0.4.0. It reads a 0x130-byte info block first, which is the 30 01 00 00 at the start of the banner. Then it checks a magic word: 0x6a6d6d05 means no password, 0x6a6d6d06 means password required. This one sends 05 6d 6d 6a. Then a 1366×768 resolution (56 05, 00 03) and screen update data. CERHOST also sends mouse and keyboard input. So this is a PLC's screen that anyone can watch and operate, with no password, on port 0.

An Omron PLC that was too busy to talk. 194.49.59.232 (AireNetworks, Spain) answered with an Omron FINS/TCP frame:

Command 1 is the server's Node Address Data Send, and error code 0x20 is "All connections are in use." It is a FINS endpoint, the protocol used to read and write Omron PLC memory, and it turned us away only because its connection slots were full. Five days later it said the same thing. A second host, 14.49.206.64 (KT, Korea), repeats an Omron Host Link frame, @FFF07F000000018C3D*, over and over. The XOR of everything before 3D is 0x3D, so the checksum is valid, but unit FF is outside the normal 00–31 range. The framing is Omron. The device behind it I couldn't pin down.

A Modbus master. 155.93.146.109 (South Africa) doesn't wait to be polled. It polls you: 10 04 00 00 00 02 72 8a is slave 16, Read Input Registers 0–1, with a valid CRC-16/MODBUS. Something out there is a SCADA master on port 0 asking whoever connects for two registers. Another host, 85.198.27.114 (Iran), repeats a 7e-framed 7-byte payload with a valid Modbus CRC, which looks like a serial-to-TCP bridge.

A power quality meter. 166.169.193.134 on Verizon Wireless answers telnet with SEL-735 Telnet Server. The SEL-735 is a Schweitzer Engineering Laboratories revenue and power quality meter, the kind utilities put on substations and feeders. This one is on a cellular modem.

Factory telemetry. 175.202.128.103 (KT, Korea) sends an HTTP POST at whoever connects:

kq-china.net is Zhejiang Keqiang Intelligent Control, which makes monitoring systems for injection moulding machines. uIP is Adam Dunkels' tiny embedded TCP/IP stack. A collector on a factory floor is pushing per-machine status to whoever connects, and masscan, confused, filed it as Minecraft.

A Swedish alarm. 82.183.26.214 (Telenor, Sweden) sends an STX/ETX frame containing "Kommunikationslarm medelvÃ¤rde", Swedish for "communication alarm, mean value." I don't know the device.

Burglar alarm communicators. Five hosts, three in Mexico and two in Spain, send a greeting of about 56 bytes, with a 2-byte length prefix up front. That matches the README of DSC-TLink, a reverse-engineered client for DSC's alarm communicators: "opening a TCP connection with the TL280 on port 3062. The TL280 will immediately send a packet of 56 bytes." The TL280 family is the IP/cellular communicator on DSC PowerSeries Neo alarm panels, and this is the DLS path used to program and monitor the panel. Here's 213.181.95.33:

(The 0a before the ICCID came out of the scan as 20, because our banner storage squashed whitespace bytes into spaces. I put it back.) The CRC-16 over the 42 bytes starting at 05 05 05 00 27 (polynomial 0xD003, from DSC-TLink's DLSProNetHeader.cs) comes out 0xea79, which is the ea 79 that follows. The MAC starts 00:03:4F, the IEEE OUI for Sur-Gard Security, DSC's receiver brand. And the device ID field on the cellular models is the SIM card's ICCID:

All three ICCIDs pass the Luhn check, and their prefixes say where the SIMs are from: 89 34 is Spain, 89 52 is Mexico. According to DSC-TLink, the AES key for the rest of the session is built from data in this greeting.

Alarm communicators that tell you the password. Three Spanish hosts, 109.167.87.236, 85.87.152.80 and 88.3.213.39, run telnet on an Alwon E20, a Notifier/Honeywell IP/GPRS alarm communicator module:

Notifier's manual agrees: telnet password, "1234 por defecto." I didn't try it.

Time clocks. 187.235.160.47 and one other host at UNINET in Mexico repeat a5 00 00 00 00 7f 00 00 e0 5c. That's an Anviz time-and-attendance frame: a5, a 4-byte device ID of 0, command 7f, zero length, and a CRC-16/MCRF4XX of 0x5ce0 sent low byte first. Open-source Anviz SDKs call 0x7f a ping.

Home automation. Five hosts answer *#*1##, the OpenWebNet ACK used by BTicino and Legrand home automation gateways: 190.113.106.159 (Costa Rica), 171.242.179.164 (Vietnam), 187.145.124.63 (Mexico), 105.157.199.125 (Morocco) and 213.7.222.134 (Cyprus). 219.241.160.119 in Korea is a SiHAS HCM-300 Ver 2.12 > prompt, a Shina System smart heating thermostat. And 192.119.63.143 (wilhelm.tel, Germany) is a mediola a.i.o. Gateway that sends its encrypted cloud request to whoever connects: POST /ra/d HTTP/1.1 host: mediola-ws.elasticbeanstalk.com.

A debug menu. Three hosts on SK Telecom in Korea, 42.42.150.116, 42.42.203.12 and 42.42.201.80, greet you with this:

192.168.225.x is the default subnet on Qualcomm LTE modules like the Quectel EC2x, so it's probably a cellular IoT device. Which product, I don't know.

A power strip. 64.201.180.254 (Gatewest, Canada) runs a Remote Power Controller v1.41i that prints its whole outlet table, circuit, name, status and boot delay, before asking for a login. Others ask only for a password but label themselves first: MGTN CO - Right Rack - Bottom, Power Switch, 151 Front Street.

SAProuters leaking their build. Four hosts are SAProuter, the SAP gateway that relays connections into SAP systems. They accepted the connection, waited 5 seconds for a route request that never came, and then sent back an NI_RTERR error packet with a lot of detail:

Every one gives away the SAProuter version (40.4), the SAP kernel release, the router's hostname, the server's local clock, and whether it's a Windows build (D:/) or not.

RustDesk. Four hosts, 93.118.140.111, 78.38.90.162 (Iran), 39.48.42.201 and 103.213.111.82 (Pakistan), open with HJ. That isn't a magic string. In RustDesk's bytes_codec.rs, the low two bits of the first byte give the header size and the rest gives the length, so 0x48 means an 18-byte message follows. 0x4a is protobuf field 9, Hash, which carries a salt and a challenge:

The salt stays the same per host. The challenge is six random characters, new on every connection. That's the password challenge a RustDesk client answers before it gets the desktop. 103.213.111.82 sends a 32-character salt, which is what newer versions use.

Winflector. Eight hosts run Winflector, a Polish remote application server. 193.32.180.51 sends Terminal client version too old - please update to version 4.0.0.1 and then an RSA public key in DER form, with exponent 17, which is Crypto++'s default. The versions are sent byte-reversed: 04 03 00 04 is 4.0.3.4. All six versions I saw are on Winflector's own version list. The hosts are in Cape Verde (156.238.40.243, 156.238.47.54 and one more), Malaysia (202.187.2.203 and one more), Poland (193.32.180.51), Ghana (80.87.66.51) and India (49.204.146.227).

VNC with no password. Of the 39 VNC servers, one doesn't ask for anything. 107.174.67.223 answers with security type none and a desktop name of resolv.moe, meow meow meow :3 haiiii!!, and an 850×591 framebuffer. Four more just send RFB 005.000.

Phone switches. 117.236.74.58 (BSNL, India) is Asterisk Call Manager/2.10.5, the Asterisk Manager Interface from Asterisk 13.26–13.34. Once logged in, AMI can originate calls and run console commands. 38.229.159.25 (France) and 41.60.171.20 (Liquid) send 04 00 fb ff LAPK, the 3CX tunnel protocol.

Cameras and NAS. exacqVision video surveillance servers on 8 hosts, including two at OneCleveland (204.156.216.130 and 204.156.198.238), send a 56-byte header ending in three values that change on every connection. Six QNAP NAS boxes expose RTRR (Realtime Remote Replication, ab ca a5 5d ...), on 202.184.138.61 in Malaysia and five others in Austria, Sri Lanka, Spain and Italy. Two Rifatron DVRs send RIFA, 42.200.149.78 in Hong Kong and 14.241.231.170 in Vietnam. One more is a UMS WebViewer DVR stream in Japan (123.242.213.10).

The rest of the RPC zoo. 223.76.20.55 (China Mobile) is ZeroC Ice, IceP 1.0 Validate Connection. 191.13.74.178 (Telefonica, Brazil) sends just ac ed 00 05, the header of a Java serialization stream, and then nothing else. 119.152.239.20 (Pakistan) sends a bare HTTP/2 SETTINGS frame with the same bytes as a default grpc-go server. 212.80.175.132 (Spain) has things backwards and sends us a full TLS 1.2 ClientHello. And 116.75.83.213 (Hathway, India) responds with RP-Version: 5.0 and RP-Application-Reason: 80108bff, which is Sony PlayStation Remote Play, most likely a PS4.

21 addresses don't wait to be asked. Connect to port 0 and they start streaming comma-separated text, about 7.5 KB of it per host in our capture:

That's SMDR (Station Message Detail Recording) output from an Avaya IP Office phone system. The fields line up with Avaya's documented layout: call start, connected time, ring time, caller, direction, called and dialled number, a call ID that starts at 1,000,000, then the two parties as E for extension or T for trunk, with names like "Line 31.1". Each host handed over 37 to 62 calls with real phone numbers, staff names, extension numbers and internal addresses. The oldest record is from December 2018. The newest is from a few days before the scan. Several hosts start with records that are years old, which I can't explain: 94.30.72.110 opened with calls from 2018-12-21, 31.10.39.182 with 2023-09-18.

Nothing was sent to get this. IP Office pushes SMDR records to whatever connects to its SMDR port, and on these 21 hosts that port is reachable on port 0. masscan stops reading at about 7.5 KB, so that's only the first few dozen calls per host.

19 of the 21 are in the UK, on BT, Wavenet, Gamma, TalkTalk, Plusnet and a handful of smaller business ISPs. One is in the US and one in South Africa. However these ended up on port 0, anyone who connects can read the company's call history.

Come say hi on the port that doesn't exist. If your client lets you.

Connect to SuperNETs on port 0 (plaintext) or port 1 (TLS) at irc.supernets.org, or reach out at acid.vegas@acid.vegas. We hang out in #war.

Making musl and proprietary NVIDIA drivers work together

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As you may be aware, one of the limitations of musl-based distributions is that they will not function to their proper extent when used in computers with various NVIDIA graphics cards.

This is due to the proprietary nature of the driver, being built for glibc. I have been experimenting with trying to make this work, and this post is the summary of that effort.

The NVIDIA graphics cards are tricky on Linux. While most other GPU vendors by default use free software graphics drivers (with kernel drivers in-tree in Linux, and userspace supplied by the Mesa project), NVIDIA in general does not, relying on an out of tree kernel driver and proprietary userland libraries that are supplied by the vendor.

A free software driver does exist, called Nouveau (kernel and OpenGL Mesa driver) and NVK (Vulkan driver). This allows the cards to work, but usually with significantly reduced performance, due to various reasons.

Currently the state depends significantly on what generation of NVIDIA card you have.

In even shorter, if you have RTX 2000 and above you are mostly okay with FOSS but lose performance, if you have newer than GTX 600 but older than GTX 1600 you are screwed and need the proprietary driver, if your card is really old it may or may not work well and you may not be able to use it at all.

We now have a pull request open for both the 580 and 620 branch. It is not yet entirely ready but works, and is available for x86_64 and aarch64 architectures as NVIDIA supplies it.

To get there, there were multiple things to take care of. Particularly, using stock musl is out of the question and will likely never work. I will elaborate on each thing.

The GLVND library was created by NVIDIA to allow for vendor-neutral OpenGL dispatch. It provides the libraries that applications link and exposes the OpenGL (and other) API. It then dispatches the calls to the implementations, which may be e.g. Mesa or the proprietary driver.

Most distributions nowadays use GLVND. Mesa comes with its own builtin dispatch code and does not necessarily require GLVND if you don’t build it with it. Musl distributions in general use this. Chimera at this point does not, and Void Linux on musl does not as it shares packaging with the glibc branch.

Chimera switched to GLVND on November 5 2026. Switching is rather straightforward and does not need much documentation. One just has to package GLVND, rebuild Mesa with it (to avoid using its builtin dispatch libraries) and take care of packages that have dependencies on mesa-devel.

GLVND is not only useful for NVIDIA drivers, but in general it is the primary thing users will find useful. There are also some vendor drivers on ARM and elsewhere but those are niche. The proprietary drivers mandate GLVND since many years back (this is also very useful on laptops with hybrid graphics, because then you may still want to keep Mesa for the iGPU).

The gcompat project provides a compatibility shim to allow for limited usage of programs linked with glibc.

Unfortunately for us, the latest tagged release of gcompat does not provide all the symbols the proprietary drivers require to resolve. Fortunately for us, the latest Git branch does. The development nowadays is very slow so we just bumped it up to the latest revision.

You can see how the packaging works in the pull request, and that part is rather unimportant for now, and does not require elaboration. It works in a similar way to other distros, downloading the .run archive from NVIDIA and installing everything to the correct places.

Of course, in our case, we cannot install it directly, because it depends on glibc and other stuff. We also can’t just pull in gcompat as is, because most of the driver is libraries that have no interpreter.

Let’s see what happens if we run ldd on libnvidia-glcore.so.580.196.07, which is the core OpenGL library.

There is a bunch of stuff here. So the libm.so.6, libc.so.6, libdl.so.2 etc resolve to ldd as they are reserved in the musl ldso:

The dynamic linker will ignore the suffixes.

For some reason, some of the libraries also have DT_NEEDED on ld-linux-x86-64.so.2 which is the glibc dynamic linker. There are also some relocation failures that we’ll get to later, for now we take out patchelf.

So, patchelf is a tool that lets you dynamically patch ELF files for things like library dependencies (DT_NEEDED), dynamic linker (DT_INTERP), RPATH, and even some funky dynamic symbol remapping.

We use this to sanitize every library and executable being installed. Let’s begin with something like the following:

What this does is drop all the dependencies we don’t need (this is optional as musl ignores them, but why not? And even on glibc since recent versions they are just compatibility symlinks that point to the libc itself) as well as add a new dependency on libgcompat.so.0 to supply the symbols.

This clears up the list a lot, and now only one error remains:

We will get to this below. You now have a rough idea about patchelf.

Googling for this tells us that it’s a relocation type for GNU indirect function (ifunc). Ifunc is a mechanism for dispatching to different versions of a function based on a runtime condition. It is done by having a resolver function which does the decision logic, and returns the implementation, which is then called. A good writeup is here.

This is intentionally not implemented in musl. In glibc it is used to pick different implementations of functions like memcpy based on CPU microarchitecture levels for performance reasons. However, indirect functions are also a major attack vector, bypassing RELRO among other things, and in general there are other ways to do runtime dispatch anyway.

I remembered that there was a project called Alpaquita Linux which is basically Alpine with performance patches. It has the musl-perf package which provides a patched musl with string functions (and other things) from glibc and for this it also has patches for ifunc. We don’t need all of them, particularly the one for static linking is useless for us, so we take the one we need and its aarch64 counterpart for a good measure.

This just adds handling of the relocation. We then package the patched libc as musl-nvidia and poof, unsupported relocation is gone.

There are now no errors in ldd. That means we’re done, right? Nope :)

So, about TLS. Normally, when you use OpenGL in an application, the implementation is dynamically loaded at runtime, not linked to the application. The OpenGL functions you use are then dynamically requested, usually through a portable wrapper (e.g. SDL_GL_GetProcAddress).

There are various TLS models. You can read up on them here. Look at the DT_FLAGS for the glcore and tls libraries. See the STATIC_TLS?

We have initial-exec, and our TLS needs to exist at a fixed offset from the thread pointer. This in general precludes dynamic allocation of the TLS memory. A quick test:

When trying to use the driver, we would get the exact same error when trying to open the GL implementation. On glibc it works because it reserves some extra space in its static TLS at the beginning and gives them out for as long as there is space. It is limited, but enough for the driver.

The correct thing here would be to use TLSDESC (-mtls-dialect=gnu2) which is efficient enough and allows for dynamic TLS allocation, but NVIDIA apparently uses some rather old toolchains.

Since we don’t want to globally preload libnvidia-glcore into each process, I ended up patching the dynamic linker to handle this scenario with the same approach. I will not elaborate on the details of the patch as you can see it in the linked pull request and it is commented. It boils down to checking if the library has DF_STATIC_TLS in DT_FLAGS during dlopen and if so, checking if we have enough space for its whole TLS image in the spare memory, and if yes, avoiding allocating dynamic TLS for it.

That allows the above example to work without LD_PRELOAD.

(Another approach could be placing the TLS symbol definitions in something that is not runtime-loaded, e.g. placed into libGL or something else, and interposing the definitions; this could theoretically avoid patching the libc for this specific thing, but we have the ifunc thing too).

I do not have any NVIDIA hardware. Before I could test, I would need to put a computer together.

Fortunately, my girlfriend still had her old computer’s GTX 980 Ti laying around. It is only supported by the legacy driver, but good enough for now. I then obtained some random old computer hardware (i3-7100, some RAM and a board, etc.)

After this, the packaging was seemingly complete and I went to do some testing on the hardware above.

Well, it turns out trying to start anything graphical would just hang. I ran ls with libnvidia-glcore preloaded and saw:

Hm, pthread_mutex_timedlock in libnvidia-gpucomp?

Inspecting the assembly revealed that it’s re-entering a lock. That suggested that this mutex should be recursive, and for some reason it is not. Then a friend remembered that glibc has static initializers for these, particularly PTHREAD_RECURSIVE_MUTEX_INITIALIZER_NP, while musl does not support static initialization, only via pthread_mutex_init, for this case.

How to deal with this? On glibc, the mutex structure is publicly visible, and has roughly this layout:

That means it’s initializing the structure with the kind field.

The mutex type is encoded in the first integer:

In this layout, the kind would look like this, mirroring the glibc structure:

I found that musl does not use this in any way, however.

We can use this. The strategy I took was creating a new shim library containing the following:

The patch_mutex_kind fetches the (on musl, unused) kind field and writes it into the type field`, atomically, if it’s unset. On statically initialized mutexes, it is always unset, as it’s only written once, and the static initializer for the default mutex just zeroes everything.

The actual encoding of the type is the same between both libcs (with the caveat that glibc also supports adaptive mutexes, which we map to regular ones), so there is not much remapping to be done. You can see the code in the pull request.

How do we use these symbols? Via patchelf again. It supports --rename-dynamic-symbols, which takes a mapping file where each line looks like oldsym newsym. We just provide ours like this:

Then we patch our libnvcompat into NEEDED of all the libraries.

There are various bits and pieces NVIDIA has released over time as actual FOSS code. This includes nvidia-xconfig, nvidia-settings, nvidia-modprobe, nvidia-persistenced, and the egl-gbm and egl-wayland external platform libraries.

These come bundled in the driver archive. Since they can be built from source, we package them separately, to minimize the amount of stuff that needs to be shimmed, and then depend on the from the driver package.

For the 620 driver, we also had to replace libstdc++.so.6 in a couple places, where it was being used for the new operator. This is provided in an way that is ABI-compatible by libc++abi.so.1 so we just replace it.

The packaging will need some more refinement, and more testing will be needed. It was reported to me that the 620 driver works with RTX 3070 Ti. It was also reported to me that it works at least partially on AArch64.

Janus Electric secures battery supply deal for its battery-swap semi truck

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Australian conversion specialists Janus Electric have been celebrating a growing slate of orders for their Class 8 electric truck conversions, which ditch diesel in favor of advanced battery-swap electric power – but exactly how they’d deliver all those trucks was still a question. Now, we have an answer: Electrovaya will supply the batteries.

The concept of dropping an electric power unit into a Class 8 semi makes perfect sense, as most of these trucks are built as gliders. That is, rolling body and chassis without an engine and transmission that’s ready to receive power from Cummins, Detroit, International, or Volvo Penta. That’s the role that Janus Electric sees itself occupying, and it seems to be working.

Janus’ reservation book crept past 70 units this past summer, helped along in part by California’s HVIP Program and the US EPA’s Diesel Emission Reduction Act (DERA) Grants. When the HVIP voucher is combined with the Port of Los Angeles’ “Plus” support, customers can received up to $166,000 per truck conversion. Factor in the massive fuel savings of electric and the classic good looks of square-nose Western Star truck body, and it’s easy to see why they’d be a popular choice.

As Jerry Seinfeld taught us however, it’s one thing to take a reservation – it’s another thing to deliver. To help make it happen, Janus has inked a battery supply deal with Electrovaya that will see a $26 million initial purchase orders for Electrovaya battery packs to demand for Janus’ products in the US and Canada, along with the battery company purchasing approximately $1.4 million in Janus stock with (what seems to be) actual money.

“Electrovaya is an important partner to Janus Electric, and we’re particularly pleased it has decided to invest directly in our business,” explains Ben Hutt, Managing Director and CEO of Janus Electric. “The investment is a strong vote of confidence in our technology and approach to electrifying heavy transport.”

The Janus Electric-repowered trucks carry a stout, 480 hp electric motor drawing power from a standard 310 kWh battery pack, with up to two battery packs (one on each side) of the truck. In practice, that gives the Janus trucks up to 370 miles of loaded range. After that, the batteries can be DC fast charged in under 90 minutes, or swapped for fresh ones in about four minutes without too much in the way of specialized equipment. That’s not just Janus’ killer app, it means that a battery supply deal like this one with a strategic partner who’s invested in the fledgling company’s success is such a huge deal.

“Reliable battery supply is critical to our growth,” adds Hutt, “and Electrovaya’s expanding North American manufacturing capabilities will be important as we scale. Both companies see considerable opportunity in this market, and we look forward to building on our partnership.”

For their part, Electrovoya executives seem to know their role here, and seem excited to be a part of what Janus is doing. “Class 8 trucking is one of the most exciting and demanding frontiers in electrification, and Janus has demonstrated real commercial traction with fleets in the US, Canada and Australia,” said Dr. Raj Das Gupta, CEO of Electrovaya. “Janus has developed a practical solution to one of the major challenges facing heavy transport, enabling diesel-powered trucks to be converted to electric without compromising their commercial operations.”

Electrovaya will be expanding its American manufacturing capacity with lithium-ion cell, module, and battery pack production at its new, potentially BABA-compliant facility in Jamestown, New York, which is expected to come online in 2027.

SOURCE | IMAGES: Janus Electric, via Charged, Yahoo.
